Abstract A simulated hospital benchmark shows that multimodal large language models have limited spatial reasoning, while fine-tuning and a 3D-computation method improve their accuracy and reduce the gap to physicians.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Spatial reasoning in hospitals requires tracking look-alike equipment, interpreting patient-relative directions, and judging passage widths, yet existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of these abilities. We introduce Clinical3D-Bench, a simulated benchmark with 3,368 questions of eight types on egocentric walkthroughs of 373 hospital-room layouts. Layouts follow hospital design guidelines, answers are computed from the 3D scenes, and paired scene states test whether models change their answers when relevant evidence changes. Videos are photorealistic, and ten physicians screened the layouts. Among 35 multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the best open and proprietary models reach 45.7% and 53.6% macro accuracy, against 40.8% for an answer prior and 79.0% for physicians on a subset, and 27 of 32 open models score below 40% on two-way patient-relative directions. Fine-tuning on Clinical3D-45K, our 44,750-question training set, lifts two MLLMs 16-20 points above the best proprietary model. 3D maps given to the fine-tuned MLLM as input go largely unused: replacing them with another video's, even when they are ground truth, barely changes its accuracy. The proposed GeoJeV instead lets the MLLM compute with 3D: it reconstructs a scene memory from each video, selects 3D operations and their arguments from the MLLM's question state, and adds their evidence to its option scores. It achieves the highest accuracy of all evaluated models, narrowing the gap to physicians.
[25.11.2026 06:29] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют Clinical3D-Bench — симулированный бенчмарк из 3368 вопросов по видеопрогулкам по 373 планировкам больничных палат, проверяющий пространственное мышление мультимодальных LLM. Лучшие модели показывают лишь около 45–54% точности против 79% у врачей, а большинство открытых моделей плохо справляются с направлениями относительно пациента. Дообучение на Clinical3D-45K заметно повышает качество, но 3D-карты почти не используются моделью. Предложенный метод GeoJeV строит 3D-память сцены, выполняет геометрические операции и добавляет их результаты к оценкам вариантов ответа, достигая лучшей точности среди всех моделей.", "emoji": "🏥", "title": "Чтобы понимать пространство больницы, LLM должна вычислять в 3D, а не только смотреть видео"}

"Abstract A simulated hospital benchmark shows that multimodal large language models have limited spatial reasoning, while fine-tuning and a 3D-computation method improve their accuracy and reduce the gap to physicians.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Spatial reasoning in hospitals requires tracking look-alike equipment, interpreting patient-relative directions, and judging passage widths, yet existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of these abilities. We introduce Clinical3D-Bench, a simulated benchmark with 3,368 questions of eight types on egocentric walkthroughs of 373 hospital-room layouts. Layouts follow hospital design guidelines, answers are computed from the 3D scenes, and paired scene states test whether models change their answers when relevant evidence changes. Videos are photorealistic, and ten physicians screened the layouts. Among 35 multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the best open and proprietary models reach 45.7% and 53.6% macro accuracy, against 40.8% for an answer prior and 79.0% for physicians on a subset, and 27 of 32 open models score below 40% on two-way patient-relative directions. Fine-tuning on Clinical3D-45K, our 44,750-question training set, lifts two MLLMs 16-20 points above the best proprietary model. 3D maps given to the fine-tuned MLLM as input go largely unused: replacing them with another video's, even when they are ground truth, barely changes its accuracy. The proposed GeoJeV instead lets the MLLM compute with 3D: it reconstructs a scene memory from each video, selects 3D operations and their arguments from the MLLM's question state, and adds their evidence to its option scores. It achieves the highest accuracy of all evaluated models, narrowing the gap to physicians."

Abstract A new benchmark shows that many robot manipulation policies perform worse when mirrors are present, often because they confuse reflections with real objects rather than simply colliding with the mirror.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Can a simple mirror expose the limitations of today's robotic manipulation policies? Inspired by the mirror self-recognition test in animal cognition, we investigate whether robot policies can reliably manipulate physical objects in scenes containing their reflections. We introduce RoboDojo-Mirror, a benchmark built on RoboDojo that preserves task objectives, success criteria, and policy checkpoints while comparing a mirror-free baseline with single-mirror, dual-mirror, and mirrored-tabletop conditions. Across five policies spanning Vision-Language-Action models, World Action Models, and agentic policies, every mirror condition reduces aggregate success rates: averaged over the three conditions, the drop ranges from 10.8 to 56.7 percentage points across policies, and it reaches 62.5 points on the mirrored tabletop. Because a mirror is also a physical obstacle, we compare it against geometrically matched non-reflective panels; 50--88\% of mirror-specific failures occur without recorded robot--panel contact, so direct collisions alone do not explain the degradation. Trajectory and contact analyses instead reveal recurring behaviors consistent with phantom targeting of reflected objects and spatial mislocalization of real ones. Targeted diagnostics further characterize grasping near reflections, policy-dependent pushing progress, and physical-target interaction under main-camera occlusion. Real-robot experiments with π_{0.5} and FastWAM also show lower overall success rates under the upright-mirror and mirrored-tabletop conditions. These findings reveal a shared vulnerability to reflective environments across diverse robot policies and motivate reflection-aware evaluation of embodied intelligence. Code is available at https://github.com/iPPLab/robodojo-mirror.
[25.11.2026 06:29] Response: {"desc": "Авторы предлагают бенчмарк RoboDojo-Mirror, который проверяет, могут ли робототехнические policy-модели (VLA, World Action Models и агентные политики) надёжно манипулировать объектами при наличии зеркал в сцене. Во всех зеркальных условиях успешность снижается, иногда на десятки процентных пунктов, причём большинство ошибок происходит без контакта робота с зеркалом, то есть дело не только в физическом препятствии. Abstract Factorized Context Attention is a training-free block-sparse attention method that natively reproduces grid probing’s span-based attention pattern, encoding input once into a KV cache to match accuracy and extend context at lower cost across video, text, and image tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Models read a book, an hour of video, or an image in one forward pass. As the context grows, this pass slows down, loses accuracy, and stops at the context window. Sparse attention is efficient, but keeps the other two limitations. Grid probing, a recent training-free method, recovers the accuracy and extends the reach by asking a frozen model the same question about short local and strided spans of the input, but every question repeats the whole computation, since each span is encoded again. Our insight is that grid probing is emulated attention, an attention pattern reproduced from outside the model through prompts, since each probe only restricts which part of the input the question can see. We introduce Factorized Context Attention (FCA), a training-free block-sparse attention that runs the same pattern natively, as the attention of the model itself. FCA encodes the input once into a KV cache, reads all spans as masked sequences in one batched forward, and uses a stair position schedule to keep every span inside the trained position range. Across long video understanding, long-context text inference and image grounding, FCA matches the accuracy and the reach of grid probing at a fraction of its cost. It gains about 10 points over the single pass on long video and 9 to 14 points on long novels, finds needles in videos of over a thousand frames and in texts four times the length of the window, and answers a later question about the same input in as little as a tenth of the time of grid probing. Native attention thus keeps what emulated attention gains without its cost, all with existing frozen language and vision-language models.
[25.11.2026 06:29] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Factorized Context Attention (FCA) — метод блочно-разреженного внимания без обучения, который встраивает паттерн grid probing непосредственно в механизм attention модели. Вход кодируется один раз в KV cache, а все локальные и страйд-спаны обрабатываются как замаскированные последовательности в одном батчевом forward-проходе с использованием stair position schedule. Такой подход сохраняет точность и дальность охвата контекста, сравнимые с grid probing, но работает существенно быстрее, в том числе при повторных вопросах к тому же входу. Метод применим к существующим замороженным LLM и VLM и показывает улучшения на длинных видео, длинных текстах и задачах grounding по изображениям.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Нативное внимание вместо имитации: точность grid probing без его цены"}

"Abstract Factorized Context Attention is a training-free block-sparse attention method that natively reproduces grid probing’s span-based attention pattern, encoding input once into a KV cache to match accuracy and extend context at lower cost across video, text, and image tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Models read a book, an hour of video, or an image in one forward pass. As the context grows, this pass slows down, loses accuracy, and stops at the context window. Sparse attention is efficient, but keeps the other two limitations. Grid probing, a recent training-free method, recovers the accuracy and extends the reach by asking a frozen model the same question about short local and strided spans of the input, but every question repeats the whole computation, since each span is encoded again. Our insight is that grid probing is emulated attention, an attention pattern reproduced from outside the model through prompts, since each probe only restricts which part of the input the question can see. We introduce Factorized Context Attention (FCA), a training-free block-sparse attention that runs the same pattern natively, as the attention of the model itself. FCA encodes the input once into a KV cache, reads all spans as masked sequences in one batched forward, and uses a stair position schedule to keep every span inside the trained position range. Across long video understanding, long-context text inference and image grounding, FCA matches the accuracy and the reach of grid probing at a fraction of its cost. It gains about 10 points over the single pass on long video and 9 to 14 points on long novels, finds needles in videos of over a thousand frames and in texts four times the length of the window, and answers a later question about the same input in as little as a tenth of the time of grid probing. Native attention thus keeps what emulated attention gains without its cost, all with existing frozen language and vision-language models."

Abstract A two-stage training approach uses visual pretext tasks to generate interleaved image-text reasoning traces and automatically built demonstrations, improving multimodal reasoning over instruction tuning alone.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash People reason with pictures as well as words. Interleaved multimodal reasoning gives models a similar way to solve problems by creating intermediate images and using them to guide further reasoning. Training models with this ability usually relies on task-specific demonstrations, which are difficult to collect. We introduce ThinkMorph-SSL, a two-stage training recipe that uses images themselves as a source of supervision. The first stage, pretext reasoning, converts visual pretext tasks, such as image colorization and denoising, into instruction-free interleaved traces. Models describe a corrupted or transformed image, generate a restored or canonical image, and interpret the result. The second stage uses automatically constructed demonstrations for question-driven instruction tuning. At the same instruction-tuning budget, models trained with pretext reasoning outperform those trained with instruction tuning alone. The full recipe improves over the base model by an average of 11.33 accuracy points across twelve benchmarks. These results show that supervision derived from images can build the visual skills needed for interleaved reasoning and improve learning from task-specific demonstrations.

Abstract A large-scale text-paired dataset of bimanual hand motion, paired with a 393-D representation and benchmark, enables systematic evaluation of generative models for dexterous motion synthesis.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash While text-driven body motion generation has matured, pure-hand synthesis remains bottlenecked by data. Existing datasets often treat hands as rigid end-effectors, lack fine-grained captions, or are too limited in scale to support robust generalization. We introduce RoMo Hands, an in-the-wild, text-paired dataset of ~542K sequences (884 hours, 95.5M frames at 30 fps) which is an order of magnitude larger than prior work. To ensure high-quality supervision, a stringent dexterity filter discards low-articulation sequences, focusing the distribution on complex bimanual movement. Each clip is paired with five captions at increasing granularities from global semantic tags to atomic finger states, organized under a hierarchical taxonomy. To bridge the gap between body and hand architectures, we propose a novel 393-D bimanual representation that adapts redundant body-motion paradigms to the high-DoF kinematics of hands. Systematic benchmarking of diffusion, masked-token, and autoregressive generators reveals distinct trade-offs under these hand-specific adaptations, including a sharp divergence between semantic alignment and kinematic plausibility. Our dataset, representation, and benchmark establish a rigorous foundation for the next generation of dexterous motion synthesis. Project page: https://romodataset.github.io/romo-hands/.

Авторы вводят 393-мерное представление двуручного движения, адаптированное под высокую степень свободы кистей, и отфильтровывают малоподвижные последовательности с помощью строгого фильтра ловкости. Систематический бенчмарк диффузионных моделей, masked-token и autoregressive генераторов показывает различия в компромиссах между семантическим соответствием текста и кинематической правдоподобностью. Abstract Group-specific prototype banks with cross-variable attention and sparse low-rank corrections improve the accuracy and interpretability of neural weather forecasting by explicitly linking local prototype contributions to prediction errors.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Data-driven weather forecasting requires distinguishing local states and their temporal evolution. However, learned patterns remain implicit in neural forecasters, making their influence difficult to interpret. Failing to distinguish local evolution can lead to similar forecasts for locations that develop differently, increasing prediction error. This motivates explicit references for distinguishing and interpreting local patterns. Prototypes provide explicit pattern references, but sample- or task-level conditioning leaves open how to adapt to distinct locations and variable groups. To deal with this problem, we design ProtoCast, which uses group-specific prototype banks to adapt local features while accounting for cross-variable dependencies. Specifically, (1) we combine each group's historical features and temporal change with forecast context from cross-variable attention for evolution-aware prototype matching. (2) Sparse mixtures of prototype-specific low-rank operators tailor corrections to each group and location. A gated attention block then exchanges the corrected features across groups before prediction. Each group's correction decomposes into explicit prototype contributions before this fusion. (3) We jointly train the forecasting network and operators from scratch and refit each group's prototypes before every training epoch. Slot tracking preserves prototype-operator associations, making each prototype's contribution to local corrections more interpretable. Experiments on GlobalForecast and TyphoonTracking show improved forecasting accuracy, while prototype deletion links local feature contributions to changes in prediction error. This work advances the development of more accurate and interpretable data-driven weather forecasting models. Project page: https://zhixuanli.github.io/projects/li2026protocast
[25.11.2026 06:30] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен ProtoCast — модель для прогнозирования погоды на основе данных, которая использует групповые банки прототипов (prototype banks), специфичные для каждой группы переменных. Прототипы сопоставляются с признаками, историей и временными изменениями с учётом контекста из cross-variable attention, что позволяет учитывать эволюцию локальных состояний. Для коррекций применяются разреженные смеси low-rank операторов, а затем gated attention объединяет скорректированные признаки между группами перед предсказанием. Благодаря slot tracking и удалению прототипов удаётся связать вклад каждого прототипа с изменением ошибки прогноза, что повышает и точность, и интерпретируемость модели.", "emoji": "🌦️", "title": "Прототипы погоды: точнее прогноз и понятное объяснение каждого вклада"}

Abstract  A single electroencephalographic (EEG) recording can support multiple decoding tasks. Fixed-label tests cannot show how EEG foundation models handle changing questions or options. We introduce EEGJev, a fast decision model that scores candidate answers in one pass. Our benchmark, EEGJev-Bench, covers six question types across 19 public EEG sources. We held the recording and options fixed while switching between questions about attributes paired during training, such as facial expression and the hand participants were asked to use. Across 13 test sources, EEGJev selected a label for the requested attribute with 99.70% accuracy (49.20% under uniform choice). Balanced accuracy for the specific answer was 62.04%. Its 95th-percentile response time was shorter than NeuroLM's on matched validation questions answered individually. EEGJev lets users switch decoding targets by changing the question. With EEGJev-Bench, researchers can test whether new decoders follow the question more reliably, identify the correct answer more often, or both.

Abstract An audit of recent image captioning evaluation benchmarks shows that duplicate samples and metric behavior inflate reported correlations, and proposes a protocol under which zero-shot vision-language judges perform best on recent test sets.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Learned image captioning metrics are trained and tested on human judgments, so their reported correlation is a claim about generalization. We audit this claim for the three most recent collections of such judgments, Polaris, Nebula, and Spica, matching images at the pixel level. First, test samples reappear in training sets: the Spica training set contains about two-thirds of the Polaris test set, with identical human ratings. Second, controlled retraining shows that duplicates can inflate a metric: for Pearl, the gain is confined to the duplicated samples and grows with every epoch; for DENEB, it stays below one point. The gain depends on the learner and on how often a training set repeats a sample. Third, Kendall's τ over all samples is the sum of two terms, detecting captions with the lowest human score and ranking the others; the first supplies most of the reported correlation and is nearly saturated. The recent test sets cannot order Polos, DENEB and Pearl consistently, and a ridge regression on frozen features matches or exceeds all three. Fourth, we propose a protocol that ranks a learned metric only with a model trained without test samples, reports both terms, and compares metrics by paired tests. Under it, zero-shot vision--language judges rank first on the three recent test sets, and a fine-tuned judge ranks first on the three earlier benchmarks. The same analysis applies to any evaluator learned from human judgments; filter lists and code are released at https://github.com/morninghut/ic-dataset-audit .
[25.11.2026 06:30] Response: {"desc": "Статья проводит аудит современных бенчмарков для оценки описаний изображений (image captioning), основанных на человеческих оценках, и показывает, что дубликаты между обучающей и тестовой выборками завышают результаты learned metrics. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract An audit of recent image captioning evaluation benchmarks shows that duplicate samples and metric behavior inflate reported correlations, and proposes a protocol under which zero-shot vision-language judges perform best on recent test sets.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Learned image captioning metrics are trained and tested on human judgments, so their reported correlation is a claim about generalization. We audit this claim for the three most recent collections of such judgments, Polaris, Nebula, and Spica, matching images at the pixel level. First, test samples reappear in training sets: the Spica training set contains about two-thirds of the Polaris test set, with identical human ratings. Second, controlled retraining shows that duplicates can inflate a metric: for Pearl, the gain is confined to the duplicated samples and grows with every epoch; for DENEB, it stays below one point. The gain depends on the learner and on how often a training set repeats a sample. Third, Kendall's τ over all samples is the sum of two terms, detecting captions with the lowest human score and ranking the others; the first supplies most of the reported correlation and is nearly saturated. The recent test sets cannot order Polos, DENEB and Pearl consistently, and a ridge regression on frozen features matches or exceeds all three. Fourth, we propose a protocol that ranks a learned metric only with a model trained without test samples, reports both terms, and compares metrics by paired tests. Under it, zero-shot vision--language judges rank first on the three recent test sets, and a fine-tuned judge ranks first on the three earlier benchmarks. The same analysis applies to any evaluator learned from human judgments; filter lists and code are released at https://github.com/morninghut/ic-dataset-audit ."

[25.11.2026 06:30] OpenRouter request. Model: anthropic/claude-haiku-5.5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. 'emoji': emoji that will reflect the theme of an article somehow, only one emoji. 'title': a slogan of a main idea of the article in Russian. Return only JSON and nothing else.

Abstract  Feedback tools and research on learner writing need transcriptions of handwritten essays that keep exactly what the student wrote: misspellings, line breaks, struck-through words and insertions. Vision-language models (VLMs) read handwriting well, but they also tend to rewrite it into correct language. We measure this on BRESSAY, 1,000 handwritten Brazilian Portuguese essays, with a benchmark that adds error preservation, invented errors, edit-markup precision and recall and line structure to the character error rate (CER) and uses paired bootstrap confidence intervals. We restrict the study to open-weight models that schools can run on their own hardware. Of 22 open baselines, the best, Qwen3-VL 235B-A22B, reaches 5.6% CER on the 199 test pages; none reproduces the edit markup reliably, and every VLM replaces 39-78% of the words absent from a Portuguese dictionary with dictionary words. We fine-tune LightOnOCR-3-1B on the 647 training pages in 85 minutes on one GPU. The resulting Escriba-OCR-1B reaches 2.06% CER, 3.5 points below the best baseline (95% CI [-4.2, -2.9]); under the ICDAR 2024 competition metric it reaches 2.1% CER, below the best published page-level results (2.2-2.4%). It recovers 67% of insertions and 77% of illegible deletions, and reproduces the line structure of 97% of the pages, but still normalises 60% of likely misspellings. Three seeds agree within 0.04 CER points, and quantised to 4 bits the model runs on an ordinary CPU at about one minute per page. On 40 letters of another corpus it matches its base model (without that model's appended comments) but misreads unseen words more often; weight interpolation recovers half the gap. A teacher's review finds that 45% of the judgeable apparent corrections are errors in the BRESSAY ground truth, so dictionary-based scores likely understate preservation. We release the model, the benchmark code and the review data.
